Corollary 60
.

Consider the conditions mentioned in Proposition
59
.
If the
f
-probability
\mathcal{P}^{f}_{0}
of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
exists, then the following hold:

1.

For any bounded and continuous function
h:\mathcal{G}_{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{\mathcal{G}_{*}}h([G,o])\ d\mathcal{P}^{f}_{0}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathcal{G}_{*}}h([G,o])d_{n}(o)\ d\mathcal{P}_{0}.

2.

For any measurable subset
A
of
\mathcal{G}_{*}
for which
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n}[A]
tends to
\mathcal{P}^{f}_{0}[A]
as
n
tends to infinity,

\mathcal{P}^{f}_{0}[A]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}_{0}\left[\frac{d_{n}(o)}{\mathcal{E}_{0}[d_{n}(o)|d_{n}(o)>0]}\mathbf{1}_{A}\ |\ d_{n}(o)>0\right].

Proof.

Follows from the definition of the
f
-probability of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
∎

7.1
Forward Backward Lemma

Consider a vertex-shift
f
and a unimodular random network
[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}]
.
Recall that the map
\theta_{f}:\mathcal{G}_{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{*}
takes
[G,o]
to
[G,f(o)]
, and the forward map
\Psi_{f}:\mathcal{G}_{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{*}
takes
[G,o]
to
[G^{f}(o),o]
, where
G^{f}(o)
is the connected component of
o
(without any loops) in the
f
-graph
G^{f}
.

We know that
\Psi_{f}([\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}])
is a unimodular Family Tree (Lemma
16
).
Consider the parent vertex-shift
F
on this Family Tree.
If
\Psi_{f}([\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}])
admits an
F
-probability, does it imply the existence and uniqueness of the
f
-probability?
The following lemma gives sufficient conditions, under which we obtain a unique
f
-probability from the
F
-probability.

Lemma 61

(Forward Backward (FB))
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a unimodular measure on
\mathcal{G}_{*}
, and
f
be a vertex-shift.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}:=\mathcal{P}\circ\theta_{f}^{-n},n\geq 1
, be the push-forward measure of
\mathcal{P}
under the
n
-th iterate of the vertex-shift map
\theta_{f}
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{P}\circ\Psi_{f}^{-1}
and
\forall n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{n}:=\mathcal{Q}\circ\theta_{F}^{-n}
be the push-forward measure of
\mathcal{Q}
under the
n
-th iterate of the Parent vertex-shift map
\theta_{F}
.

Suppose there exists a map
\Phi_{f}:\mathcal{T}_{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{*}
and a probability measure
\mathcal{Q}_{F}
on
\mathcal{G}_{*}^{\prime}
such that

1.

\Phi_{f}\circ\Psi_{f}=I
,
\mathcal{P}
-a.s., where
Id
is the identity map.

2.

\mathcal{Q}_{F}
is the
F
-probability of
\mathcal{Q}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{F}
weakly as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

3.

\Phi_{f}
is continuous
\mathcal{Q}_{F}
-a.s..

Then,
\mathcal{Q}_{F}\circ\Phi_{f}^{-1}
is the
f
-probability of
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

Observe that
\Psi_{f}\circ\theta_{f}=\theta_{F}\circ\Psi_{f}
. Indeed, for any
[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{f}\circ\theta_{f}([G,o])

\displaystyle=\Psi_{f}([G,f(o)])=[G^{f}(f(o)),f(o)]

\displaystyle=[G^{f}(o),f(o)]=[G^{f}(o),F(o)]=\theta_{F}\circ\Psi_{f}([G,o]).

The third equality in the above equation follows from the fact that the connected component of
o
in
G^{f}
is the same as that of
f(o)
.
Using this inductively we obtain, for any
n\geq 1
,

\mathcal{Q}_{n}=\mathcal{Q}\circ\theta_{F}^{-n}=\mathcal{P}\circ\Psi_{f}^{-1}\circ\theta_{F}^{-n}=\mathcal{P}\circ\theta_{f}^{-n}\circ\Psi_{f}^{-1}=\mathcal{P}_{n}\circ\Psi_{f}^{-1}.

We know that
\mathcal{Q}
is unimodular because
\mathcal{P}
is unimodular by Lemma
16
.
By Lemma
58
,
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{n}
) is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}
) for all
n\geq 1
.
So, it follows from the first condition that
\Phi_{f}\circ\Psi_{f}=I

\mathcal{P}_{n}
-a.s..
Using this, we obtain

\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{n}\circ(\Phi_{f}\circ\Psi_{f})^{-1}=\mathcal{P}_{n}\circ\Psi_{f}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{f}^{-1}=\mathcal{Q}_{n}\circ\Phi_{f}^{-1}.

Since
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
weakly converges to
\mathcal{Q}_{F}
and
\Phi_{f}
is continuous
\mathcal{Q}_{F}
-a.s., by the Mann-Wald theorem
[
9
]
,
\mathcal{Q}_{n}\circ\Phi_{f}^{-1}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{Q}_{F}\circ\Phi_{f}^{-1}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Thus,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{Q}_{F}\circ\Phi_{f}^{-1}
.
∎

In general, the existence of the map
\Phi_{f}
is not guaranteed since the
f
-graph may not contain the needed information about the structure of the network.
For instance, consider the identity vertex-shift.
In this case, the connected components of its
f
-graph are just singletons.
So, no information can be obtained from the
f
-graph.
We may however attach more information of a network to its
f
-graph as marks.
In the case of the record vertex-shift
R
on the random network
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
(where
X
is the sequence of i.i.d. increments of a skip-free to the left random walk), it suffices to consider the order of
\mathbb{Z}
when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
and both the order and the type function
t
when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]>0
as we shall see in the next chapter.
Note that, in FB Lemma
61
, the backward map
\Phi_{f}
may depend on the initial distribution
\mathcal{P}
.

8
Finite orbits and relative compactness

We use the notations of Subsection
1.1
.
In this section, we are only focused on unlabelled graphs.
Recall that
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}
denotes the space of rooted graphs.

Definition 62
.

A vertex-shift
f
is said to have
finite orbits
on a random graph
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
if the set
\{[G,f^{n}(o)],n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is finite for
-\mathcal{P}_{0}
-a.s.
[G,o]\in\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is the probability measure induced by
[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}]
.

Proposition 63
.

Let
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
be a random graph which induces the probability measure
\mathcal{P}_{0}
on
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}
,
f
be a vertex-shift and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}=\{\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
be the vertex-shift probabilities induced by
f
. If
f
has finite orbits on
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
then
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}
is relatively compact.

Proof.

Let
r\geq 1
be fixed. We show that,

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}[[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]_{r}\not\in K_{m}^{r}]=0.

(8.1)

For any fixed
m\geq 1
and for all
n\geq 1
, the event
\{[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]_{r}\in\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r}\}
is a subset of
\{orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}\cap(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r})\not=\emptyset\}
, where
orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}=\{[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]_{r},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
This implies that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}[[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]_{r}\in\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r}]\leq\mathbb{P}[orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}\cap(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r})\not=\emptyset].

Since
K_{m}^{r}
is an increasing sequence in
m
,
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r}
is a decreasing sequence of sets, which implies that the events
\{orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}\cap(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r})\not=\emptyset\}
are decreasing in
m
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle[[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]_{r}\in\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r}]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}[orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}\cap(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r})\not=\emptyset]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\{orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}\cap(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r})\not=\emptyset\}\right].

But
orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}
is a.s. finite. So, it can have non-empty intersection with
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r}
only for finitely many
m
.
Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\left[\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\{orb([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])_{r}\cap(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{*}\backslash K_{m}^{r})\not=\emptyset\}\right]=0,

which gives the desired equality in Equation (
8.1
).
∎

9
Evaporation

The
n
-th vertex-shift-probability measure
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n}
of a probability measure
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is supported on the rooted network
[G,o]
whose root
o
has
n
-th descendants, i.e.,
d_{n}(o)>0
.
This leads us to ask, “are all
f
-probabilities, assuming they exist, supported only on the rooted networks whose root has infinitely many descendants, i.e.,
d_{n}(o)>0,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
?”.
The answer is affirmative under the sufficient conditions mentioned in Lemma
65
or in Lemma
66
.

Let us denote
I:=\{[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}\text{ such that }d_{n}(o)>0\ \forall n\geq 1\}
, the set of rooted networks whose root has infinitely many descendants.
Let
A_{m}=\{[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}\text{ such that }d_{m}(o)=0\}
and
J=\cup_{m\geq 1}A_{m}
. Then,
J
is the set of rooted networks whose root has finitely many descendants, and we have

J^{c}=\cap_{m\geq 1}A_{m}^{c}=\cap_{m\geq 1}\{[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}:d_{m}(o)>0\}=I.

Definition 64
.

We say that a random network
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]

evaporates
on a vertex-shift
f
if
\mathbb{P}[[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]\in J]=1
.

Lemma 65
.

Let
f
be a vertex-shift and
[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}]
be a unimodular random network.
If
Q
is an
f
-probability of
[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}]
, then
Q[I]=1
.

Proof.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let us denote
B_{n}=\{[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}\text{ such that }d_{n}(o)>0\}
.
Then
B_{n}
is a non-increasing sequence of subsets whose intersection is
I
, i.e.,
I=\cap_{n}B_{n}
and
B_{n}\searrow I
.
Therefore,

\mathcal{Q}[I]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{Q}[B_{n}].

Since
\mathcal{Q}
is a
f
-probability, there exists a subsequence
\{\mathcal{P}^{f,n_{k}}_{0}\}
of the vertex-shift probabilities
\{\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n}\}
that converges weakly to
\mathcal{Q}
.
Equivalently, for any continuity set
B
,
\mathcal{P}^{f,n_{k}}_{0}[B]\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}[B]
as
k
tends to infinity.

For all
n\geq 1
, the map
[G,o]\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{d_{n}(o)>0}
is continuous on
\mathcal{G}_{*}
since for any sequence
([G_{m},o_{m}])_{m\geq 1}
that converges to a network
[G^{\prime},o^{\prime}]
as
m\to\infty
, the sequence
(d_{n}(o_{m}))_{m\geq 1}
is eventually a constant sequence,i.e., there exists
k\geq 1
such that
d_{n}(o_{m})=d_{n}(o^{\prime})
for all
m\geq k
.
Therefore,
B_{n}
is a
\mathcal{Q}
-continuity set, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}^{f,n_{k}}_{0}[B_{n}]\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}[B_{n}]
as
k
tends to infinity.
But, notice that
\mathcal{P}^{f,n_{k}}_{0}[B_{n_{k}}]=1
, as it is only supported on the roots that have
n_{k}
-th descendants, and
B_{n_{k}}\subseteq B_{n}
for all
n_{k}\geq n
.
So,
\mathcal{P}^{f,n_{k}}_{0}[B_{n}]=1
for all
n_{k}\geq n
which implies that
\mathcal{Q}[B_{n}]=1
.
This gives the desired result that
\mathcal{Q}[I]=1
.

∎

Lemma 66
.

Let
f
be a vertex-shift and
\mathcal{Q}
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{G}_{*}
which satisfies Mecke’s invariant measure equation, i.e.,
(\theta_{f})^{*}\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}
. Then
\mathcal{Q}[I]=1
.

Proof.

We show that
Q[J]=0
.
For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let us denote
A_{m}=\{[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}:d_{m}(o)=0\}
.
Since
(\theta_{f})_{*}\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}
, we have
(\theta^{m}_{f})_{*}\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}[A_{m}]

\displaystyle=(\theta^{m}_{f})_{*}\mathcal{Q}[A_{m}]=\mathcal{Q}[(\theta_{f}^{m})^{-1}(A_{m})]=\mathcal{Q}\left[[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}:[G,f^{m}(o)]\in A_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=0\text{ (since no element of $A_{m}$ has $m$-th descendant).}

Since
\mathcal{Q}[A_{m}]=0
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\mathcal{Q}[J]=\mathcal{Q}\left[\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}A_{m}\right]=0.

Therefore,
\mathcal{Q}[I]=1
.
∎

Corollary 67
.

Let
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
be a random network which evaporates under the action of a vertex-shift
f
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
be a
f
-probability of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
(the distribution of
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
) which satisfies Mecke’s invariant measure equation, i.e.,
(\theta_{f})^{*}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}=\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
. Then,
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
is singular with respect to the distribution
\mathcal{P}_{0}
of
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
.

Proof.

Since
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
evaporates under
f
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}[J]=1
.
By Lemma
66
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}[J]=0
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
is singular with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{o}
.
∎

Corollary 68
.

Let
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
be a random network which evaporates under the action of a vertex-shift
f
. Assume that the vertex-shift probabilities converge to a unique
f
-probability
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
and
\theta_{f}
is continuous at
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
. Then,
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
is singular with respect to the distribution
\mathcal{P}_{0}
of
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
.

Proof.

Let
\{\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n}\}
be the vertex-shift-probabilities of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
Since, for all
n\geq 1
,
(\theta_{f})_{*}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n}=\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n+1}
, the sequence
((\theta_{f})_{*}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f,n})_{n}
converges to
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
.
Since
\theta_{f}
is continuous at
\mathcal{P}_{o}^{f}
, we have
(\theta_{f})_{*}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}=\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
.
The statement follows from Corollary
67
.
∎

10
Periodicity

In the previous Section, we saw conditions under which an
f
-probability is singular with respect to the initial distribution.
In this section, we show that if the vertex-shift is periodic then
f
-probabilities are absolutely continuous with respect to the initial distribution (see Theorem
72
, Remark
74
).

Definition 69
.

A vertex-shift
f
is said to be
periodic
if for all
G
and
o\in V(G)
, there exists positive integers
k=k(o)
and
p=p(o)
such that

\forall n\geq k,\ f_{G}^{n+p}(o)=f^{n}(o).

Definition 70
.

A vertex-shift
f
is said to be
p
-periodic
if
p
does not depend on
[G,o]
.

A vertex
o\in V(G)
is called a
trap
of
G
with respect to a vertex-shift
f
if
f_{G}(o)=o
.
For the identity vertex-shift, every vertex of every network is a trap.
Note that, for any vertex-shift
f
, the collection of set of traps of networks
S_{G}:=\{u\in V(G):f_{G}(u)=u\}
is a covariant subset.

Lemma 71
.

If a vertex-shift
f
is
1
-periodic a.s. on a random network
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
then the limit
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]
exists and is supported on the traps, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}[\lim_{n\to\infty}[\mathbf{G},f^{n}(\mathbf{o})]\in B]=1
, where
B:=\{[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}:o\text{ is a trap}\}
.

Proof.

Since
f
is
1
-periodic a.s., for
\mathcal{P}_{0}
-a.s.
[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}
there exists
k=k(o)
such that

\forall n\geq k,f_{G}^{n+1}(o)=f_{G}^{n}(o)=f_{G}^{k}(o).

Therefore,
f_{G}^{k}(o)
is a trap. Hence,
\mathbb{P}[\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]\in B]=1
.
∎

In view of the above lemma, for a random network
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
and a
1
-periodic vertex-shift
f
, let
[\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\infty}
be the distribution of
[\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}]
.

Theorem 72
.

Let
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
be a random network,
\mathcal{P}_{0}
be its distribution and
f
be an a.s.
1
-periodic vertex-shift on
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
.
Then the
f
-probability
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
exists and
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
is the distribution of
[\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}]
.

In addition, if
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
is unimodular then the
f
-probability
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the initial distribution
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
The limit function
d_{\infty}(o)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{n}(o)
exists and it is finite for
\mathcal{P}_{o}^{f}
-a.s.
[G,o]
.
The Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
is given by

\frac{d\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}}{d\mathcal{P}_{0}}[G,o]=d_{\infty}(o)\mathbf{1}\{\text{o is a trap}\}.

Proof.

We prove that
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\infty}
(the distribution of
[\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}]
) is the
f
-probability of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
For any bounded and continuous function
h:\mathcal{G}_{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{G}_{*}}h[G,o]\ d\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h[\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}]]=\mathbb{E}[h(\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})])]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}h[\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})]]\text{ (by continuity of $h$)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[h([\textbf{G},f^{n}(\textbf{o})])].

In the last step of the above equation, we used the boundedness of
h
and the dominated convergence theorem.
Thus,
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\infty}
is the
f
-probability of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.

We now assume that
[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}]
is unimodular.

The limit function
d_{\infty}
:

The sequence of functions
(d_{n}(o))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-decreasing on the set of traps due to the following reason.
Let
o\in V(G)
be a trap, and
u\in D_{n}(o)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, since
f_{G}^{n+1}(u)=f_{G}^{n}(u)=o
, we have
D_{n}(o)\subseteq D_{n+1}(o)
and
d_{n}(o)\leq d_{n+1}(o)
.

Since the random network
[\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}]
is supported on the traps, the random variable

d_{\infty}([\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}])=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{n}([\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}])

exists a.s.

As for the finiteness of the random variable
d_{\infty}([\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}])
, let

\mathcal{A}:=\{[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}:f_{G}^{n+1}(o)=f_{G}^{n}(o)\text{ for some $n=n(o)\in\mathbb{N}$ }\}.

Then, for any
[G,o]\in\mathcal{A}
, the root
o
has finitely many ancestors in the
f
-graph.
Therefore, by the unimodular classification theorem (
[
7
]
), the connected component
C(o)
of
o
is of type
\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{F}
as it is the only case in which vertices have finitely many ancestors.
This implies that
C(o)
is finite.
But the connected component of
f^{n(o)}(o)
in the
f
-graph is also
C(o)
and
f^{n(o)}(o)
is a trap.
Thus,
d_{\infty}(\lim_{m\to\infty}[G,f^{m}(o)])=\#D(f^{n(o)}(o))=\#C(o)
is finite.

Radon-Nikodym derivation of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
:

Let
h:\mathcal{G}_{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be any measurable function. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{G}_{*}}

\displaystyle h([G,o])\ d\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}=\mathbb{E}[h([\bar{\textbf{G}},\bar{\textbf{o}}])]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in V(\bar{\textbf{G}})}h([\bar{\textbf{G}},u])\mathbf{1}\{\bar{\textbf{o}}=u\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in V(\textbf{G})}h([\textbf{G},u])\mathbf{1}\{f^{n(\textbf{o})}(\textbf{o})=u\}\mathbf{1}\{f^{n(\textbf{o})}(\textbf{o})\text{ is a trap}\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in V(\textbf{G})}h([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])\mathbf{1}\{f^{n(u)}(u)=\textbf{o}\}\mathbf{1}\{f^{n(u)}(u)\text{ is a trap}\}\right]

(10.1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[h([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])\ \#D(\textbf{o})\mathbf{1}\{\textbf{o}\text{ is a trap}\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[h([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}d_{m}(\textbf{o})\mathbf{1}\{\textbf{o}\text{ is a trap}\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h([\textbf{G},\textbf{o}])\ d_{\infty}(\textbf{o})\mathbf{1}\{\textbf{o}\text{ is a trap}\}].

We used unimodularity to get Eq. (
10.1
).
Thus, we obtain the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}
with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
,

\frac{d\mathcal{P}_{0}^{f}}{d\mathcal{P}_{0}}[G,o]=d_{\infty}(o)\mathbf{1}\{\text{o is a trap}\}.

∎

Remark 73
.

From the proof of Theorem
72
, it follows that
d_{\infty}(\mathbf{o})=\#D(\mathbf{o})
.

Remark 74
.

Let
p
be a positive integer.
If a vertex-shift
f
is a.s.
p
-periodic on a random network
[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}]
then for
\mathcal{P}_{0}
-a.s.
[G,o]\in\mathcal{G}_{*}
, there exists a
k=k(o)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f_{G}^{n+p}(o)=f_{G}^{n}(o)
for all
n\geq k
.
In particular,
f_{G}^{np+i+p}(o)=f_{G}^{np+i}(o)
for all
n\geq k
and for any
i=0,1,\cdots,p-1
.
So, the vertex-shift
f^{p}
is a.s.
1
-periodic on the random networks
\{[\textbf{G},\textbf{o}],[\textbf{G},f(\textbf{o})],\cdots,[\textbf{G},f^{p-1}(\textbf{o})]\}
.
By Theorem
72
, for each
i=0,1,\cdots,p-1
,
[\mathbf{G},f^{i}(\mathbf{o})]
has unique
f^{p}
-probability.
So,
[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}]
can have at most
p

f
-probabilities.
In addition, if
[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{o}]
is unimodular, then all of its
f
-probabilities are absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
by Lemma
58
and by Theorem
72
.

11
Examples and their F-probabilities

In this section, we compute the
F
-probabilities of some Family Trees.
A few remarks are given in the respective subsections about the application of results of the previous section to the examples studied here.
Among the examples, the Canopy tree and the unimodular Eternal Galton-Watson Tree are taken from
[
7
]
.
We have already introduced two new unimodular Family Trees namely, the typically rooted Galton-Watson Tree and the unimodularised bi-variate Eternal Kesten Tree.
The
F
-probability of the latter is shown to be the bi-variate Eternal Kesten Tree in Proposition
82
.
We introduce Eternal Kesten Tree, a special instance of bi-variate Eternal Kesten Tree, and prove that it is the
F
-probability of the Eternal Galton-Watson Tree (in Proposition
80
).
We also introduce the size-biased Galton-Watson Tree, and show that it is the
F
-probability of the typically rooted Galton-Watson Tree.

The results from this section together with FB Lemma
61
will be used in the next chapter to compute the
R
-probability.

11.1
Canopy tree

The Canopy tree with the number of children d,
C_{d}
is an Eternal Family Tree with vertices partitioned into infinitely many levels
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},\cdots
such that, for any
i>0
, every vertex
v
in
L_{i}
has
d
children in
L_{i-1}
, and a unique parent in
L_{i+1}
; the vertices of
L_{0}
do not have any children but each vertex of
L_{0}
has a unique parent in
L_{1}
.
The tree is deterministic, but the root is chosen randomly to obtain a random rooted Family Tree
[C_{d},\mathbf{o}]
.

Since, for any two vertices
u,v
of the same level
L_{i}
(for any
i\geq 0
), the equivalent class of
(C_{d},u)
and
(C_{d},v)
are one and the same, the set of probabilities
\{p_{i}=\mathbb{P}[\textbf{o}\in L_{i}]:i\geq 1\}
fully characterizes the distribution of
[C_{d},\mathbf{o}]
.
Thus, in order to describe the distribution of the random root
o
of
C_{d}
, it is enough to define
\mathbb{P}[\textbf{o}\in L_{i}]
.
Let

p_{i}=\mathbb{P}[\textbf{o}\in L_{i}],\ i\geq 0.

Let
T_{d+1}
be the directed tree whose vertices have one out-degree and
d
in-degree, i.e.,
T_{d+1}
is the
d+1
regular tree with one selected end.
Consider the parent vertex-shift on
C_{d}
.
In Proposition
76
, we show that the randomly rooted Canopy tree
[C_{d},\mathbf{o}]
has a unique
F
-probability
T_{d+1}
.

Recall the notations of Section
1
of Chapter
2
.

Lemma 75
.

Let
u
be a vertex of the canopy tree
C_{d}
that belongs to level
L_{i}
.
Then

((C_{d},u)_{r},u)\sim((T_{d+1},v)_{r},v)\text{ for all }0\leq r\leq i.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on
r
.
For
r=0
,
((C_{d},u)_{r},u)\sim((T_{d+1},v)_{r},v)
.
Let us assume that the statement is true for
r-1\geq 0
and let
\rho:(C_{d},u)_{r-1}\rightarrow(T_{d+1},v)_{r-1}
be a network isomorphism such that
\rho(u)=v
.
To show
((C_{d},u)_{r},u)\sim((T_{d+1},v)_{r},v)
, it is enough to extend the isomorphism
\rho
to every vertex in
(C_{d},u)_{r}\backslash(C_{d},u)_{r-1}
.

If
w\in(C_{d},u)_{r}\backslash(C_{d},u)_{r-1}
then
w
can only be one of the three cases: an ancestor of
u
, or a descendant of
u
, or a descendant of an ancestor of
u
.

•

If
w
is an ancestor of
u
then
w=F^{r}(u)
and
F^{r-1}(u)\in(C_{d},u)_{r-1}
.
We extend
\rho
to
w
by assigning
\rho(w)
to the parent of
\rho(F^{r-1}(u))
in
T_{d+1}
.

•

If
w
is a descendant either of
u
or, of an ancestor of
u
then
F(w)\in(C_{d},u)_{r-1}
.
As
i\geq r
, the vertex
F(w)\not\in L_{0}
.
Therefore,
F(w)
has
d
-children and we can obtain a bijection between the children of
F(w)
and the children of
\rho(F(w))
.
We extend
\rho
to the children of
F(w)
by using this bijection.

∎

Let
[C_{d},u_{i}]
be the equivalence class of any
u_{i}
vertex in
L_{i}
.
By the above lemma, the distance between
[C_{D},u_{i}]
and
[T_{d+1},v]
in
\mathcal{T}_{*}
is bounded above by
\frac{1}{2^{i}}
.
Thus,
[C_{d},u_{i}]\rightarrow[T_{d+1},v]
in
\mathcal{G}_{*}
as
i
tends to infinity.
We now show that the
F
-probability of a randomly rooted Canopy tree
[C_{d},\mathbf{o}]
is
[T_{d+1},v]
, for any vertex
v
of
T_{d+1}
.

Proposition 76
.

Let
[C_{d},\textbf{o}]
be the canopy tree with
o
the root
\mathbf{o}
chosen randomly
and
[C_{d},\textbf{o}_{i}]\in\mathcal{T}_{*}
be the canopy tree with random root
\textbf{o}_{i}
, where
\textbf{o}_{i}=F^{i}(\textbf{o}),\ i\geq 0
.
Then
[C_{d},\textbf{o}_{i}]
converges in distribution to
[T_{d+1},v]
as
i\to\infty
, for any vertex
v
of
T_{d+1}
.

Proof.

Let g be a continuous bounded function on
\mathcal{G}_{*}
,
u_{k}\in L_{k}
be a vertex of
C_{d}
,
g_{k}:=g([C_{d},u_{k}])
, and
a:=g([T_{d+1},v])
.
As
[C_{d},u_{k}]\rightarrow[T_{d},v]
in
\mathcal{G}_{*}
when
k
tends to infinity, and by the continuity of the function
g
,
g_{k}
converges to
a
.

The probability,
\mathbb{P}[\textbf{o}_{i}\in L_{k}]=\mathbb{P}[F^{i}(\textbf{o})\in L_{k}]=\mathbb{P}[\textbf{o}\in L_{k-i}]
is given by

\mathbb{P}[\textbf{o}_{i}\in L_{k}]=\begin{cases}0&k-i<0\\
p_{k-i}&k-i\geq 0.\end{cases}

and we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[g([C_{d},\textbf{o}_{i}])]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}g([C_{d},u_{k}])\mathbb{P}[\textbf{o}_{i}\in L_{k}]=\sum_{k=i}^{\infty}g_{k}p_{k-i}.

Since
g_{i}
converges to
a
as
i
tends to infinity, given
\epsilon>0
there exists
N
such that for all
i\geq N
,
|g_{i}-a|<\epsilon
.
Therefore, for such
i
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}[g([C_{d},\textbf{o}_{i}])]-g([T_{d+1},v])\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{k=i}^{\infty}g_{k}p_{k-i}-a\right|=\left|p_{0}(g_{i}-a)+p_{1}(g_{i+1}-a)+\cdots\right|

\displaystyle\leq p_{0}|g_{i}-a|+p_{1}|g_{i+1}-a|+\cdots+p_{k}|g_{i+k}-a|+\cdots

\displaystyle<\epsilon\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{k}\right)<\epsilon.

∎

Remark 77
.

Observe that the map
\theta_{F}
induced by the parent vertex-shift
F
is continuous on
\mathcal{T}_{*}
.
It is also clear that the randomly rooted Canopy tree
[C_{d},\mathbf{o}]
evaporates under
F
as the root
\mathbf{o}
has a.s. finitely many descendants.
Therefore, by Corollary
68
, the
F
-probability of
[C_{d},\mathbf{o}]
is singular with respect to the distribution of
[C_{d},\mathbf{o}]
.
This is indeed true since the directed regular tree
[T_{d+1},u]
has infinitely many descendants.

11.2
Typically rooted Galton-Watson Tree (TGWT)

Let
\pi
be a probability distribution on
\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
with mean
m(\pi)<1
and
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}]
be a random Family Tree distributed as
GW(\pi)
.
The
Size-Biased Galton-Watson Tree
with offspring distribution
\pi
, denoted as
SBGW(\pi)
is a random Family Tree
[\bar{\mathbf{T}},\bar{\mathbf{o}}]
whose distribution is given by:

\mathbb{P}[[\bar{\mathbf{T}},\bar{\mathbf{o}}]\in A]=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})]}\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}])],

(11.1)

for every measurable set
A
.
Note that
\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T})]
is finite since
m(\pi)<1
.

We show that the
F
-probability of
TGWT(\pi)
is
SBGW(\pi)
using Theorem
72
.

Proposition 78
.

Let
m(\pi)<1
.
Then, the
F
-probability of
TGWT(\pi)
is
SBGW(\pi)
.

Proof.

Let
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
be a random Family Tree distributed as
TGWT(\pi)
and let
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}]
be a random Family Tree distributed as
GW(\pi)
.
Since, the root
\mathbf{o}
has a.s. finitely many ancestors,
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
is a.s.
1
-periodic with respect to the parent vertex-shift
F
.
By Theorem
72
, the
F
-probability of
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
exists.
Let
[\bar{\mathbf{T}},\bar{\mathbf{o}}]
be the
F
-probability of
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
.
Since
TGWT(\pi)
is unimodular (see Proposition
52
), by Theorem
72
, we have

\mathbb{P}[[\bar{\mathbf{T}},\bar{\mathbf{o}}]\in A]=\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T})\mathbf{1}\{\mathbf{o}\text{ is a trap }\}\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}])],

for every measurable set
A
.
By Proposition
50
, we have

\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]\in A]=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})]}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})}\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T}^{\prime},u])\right],

for every measurable set
A
.
Combining the above two equations, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[[\bar{\mathbf{T}},\bar{\mathbf{o}}]\in A]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})]}\mathbb{E}\left[\#V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})\sum_{u\in V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})}\mathbf{1}\{\mathbf{u}\text{ is a trap }\}\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T}^{\prime},u])\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})]}\mathbb{E}\left[\#V(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}])\right],

for every measurable set
A
.
The last step follows since the root is the only vertex that does not have a parent and thus the statement is proved.
∎

11.3
Unimodular Eternal Galton-Watson Tree

In the following, we introduce a new EFT called the Eternal Kesten Tree (
EKT
) and show that it is the
F
-probability of the unimodular Eternal Galton-Watson Tree.
The
EKT
is an eternal version of Kesten’s tree which was defined in
[
1
]
.

Let
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
be a rooted Family Tree.
A vertex of
\mathbf{T}
is called
common(
\pi
)
if its offspring distribution is
\pi
and all of its descendants reproduce independently with offspring distribution
\pi
.
A vertex of
\mathbf{T}
is
special(
\pi
)
if it satisfies the following three properties: (1) its offspring distribution is
\hat{\pi}
(the size-biased distribution of
\pi
), (2) all of its descendants reproduce independently, (3) for all
n\geq 1
, exactly one of its
n
-th descendants reproduces with distribution
\hat{\pi}
and the rest (if any) are common(
\pi
).

The ordered Eternal Kesten Tree:

Let
\pi
be a probability distribution on
\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
with mean
m(\pi)=1
.
The ordered Eternal Kesten Tree with offspring distribution
\pi
, denoted as
EKT(\pi)
, is defined as follows: it is a rooted Eternal Family Tree
[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{u}]
containing two types of individuals (vertices) namely common(
\pi
) and special(
\pi
).
It has a unique bi-infinite
F
-path
(\mathbf{o}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of special(
\pi
) individuals with
\mathbf{o}_{0}=\mathbf{u}
, i.e.,
\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbf{o}_{n}
is a parent of
\mathbf{o}_{n-1}
and
\mathbf{o}_{n-1}
is the special(
\pi
) child of
\mathbf{o}_{n}
.
All the remaining vertices are common(
\pi
).
The order among the children of every vertex is uniform.
All individuals reproduce independently.

The relation between the Eternal Kesten Tree and the bi-variate Eternal Kesten Tree (defined in Subsection
5.1.2
) is as follows: Let
\pi
be a probability distribution on
\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
with mean
m(\pi)=1
,
\hat{\pi}
be the size-biased distribution of
\pi
, and
\alpha
be the distribution given by
\alpha(k)=\hat{\pi}(k+1)
for all
k\geq 0
.
Then, the unordered
EKT(\pi)
is the same as the unordered
EKT(\alpha,\pi)
, and the ordered
EKT(\pi)
is obtained by taking the uniform order on
EKT(\alpha,\pi)
.

Figure 9:
The Eternal Kesten tree with distribution
\pi
. Notation:
GW(\pi)
denotes Galton-Watson tree;
\hat{\pi}
is the size-biased distribution of
\pi
.

Remark 79
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the descendant trees of siblings of
\mathbf{o}_{n}
are independent critical Galton-Watson trees with offspring distribution
\pi
, and thus they are all finite trees.
Therefore, every realization of
EKT(\pi)
has a unique bi-infinite
F
-path passing through the root. See Figure
9
for an illustration.

We follow the notations of Section
1
, Chapter
2
.

Proposition 80
.

The
F
-probability of the unimodular (ordered) Eternal Galton-Watson Tree
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
with offspring distribution
\pi
is the (ordered) Eternal Kesten Tree
[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{u}]
with distribution
EKT(\pi)
.

Proof.

Let
r>0
, and
\mathbf{u}_{-r},\mathbf{u}_{-r+1},\cdots,\mathbf{u}_{-1}
be the special descendants of
\mathbf{u}_{0}=\mathbf{u}
in
\mathbf{S}
.
Note that
F^{n}(\mathbf{o})
has at least one
k
-th descendant, for all
1\leq k\leq n
.
For all
n>r
, the distributions of
[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o})]_{r}
and
[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{u}]_{r}
are the same.
Indeed, the offspring distribution of
F^{n}(\mathbf{o})
is the same as that of
\mathbf{u}
which is
\hat{\pi}
.
The special child of
\mathbf{u}
has the same offspring distribution
\hat{\pi}
as that of
F^{n-1}(\mathbf{o})
and the remaining other children of
u
follow the offspring distribution
\pi
.
By induction,
F^{n-r+1}(\mathbf{o})
has the same distribution as that of the special
n-r+1
-th descendant of
\mathbf{u}
.
The same is true for the remaining vertices of
[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o})]_{r}
and
[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{u}]_{r}
.
Therefore,

[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{u}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o})].

In addition, if
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
is an ordered
EGWT(\pi)
and
[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{u}]
is an ordered
EKT(\pi)
, then the order of
F^{i}(\mathbf{o})
is uniform among the children of
F^{i+1}(\mathbf{o})
for
n-r+1\leq i\leq n-1
, which has the same distribution as the order of
u_{i-n}
among the children of
u_{i-n+1}
.
The same is true for the order of the remaining vertices of
[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o})]_{r}
and
[\mathbf{S},u]_{r}
.
Thus, the
F
-probability of the unimodular ordered
EGWT(\pi)
is the ordered
EKT(\pi)
.
∎

Remark 81
.

Consider a realization of
EGWT(\pi)
, where
\pi
has mean
m(\pi)=1
and satisfies
\pi(1)<1
.
Then, its root has finitely many descendants, i.e.,
EGWT(\pi)
evaporates under the parent vertex-shift.
In contrast, the root of a realization of
EKT(\pi)
has infinitely many descendants.
Therefore,
EKT(\pi)
is
singular
with respect to
EGWT(\pi)
.
This is another instance where the
F
-probability is singular with respect to the initial distribution.
This fact can also be inferred from Lemma
66
and Corollary
68
, since the parent vertex-shift is continuous on
\mathcal{T}_{*}
.

11.4
Unimodularised bi-variate marked Eternal Kesten Tree

Let
\alpha,\beta
be probability distributions on
\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
such that their means satisfy
m(\alpha)<\infty
and
m(\beta)<1
.
Let
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]
be a random marked Family Tree whose distribution is
MEKT(\alpha,\beta)
, where
Z^{\prime}
is a mark.
Let
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]
be the joining of
([\mathbf{T}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i},Z_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
, the i.i.d. sequence of random marked Family Trees defined in Remark
43
.

Let
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o},Z]
denote the size-biased joining of
([\mathbf{T}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i},Z_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
Recall that the distribution of
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o},Z]
is given by:

\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o},Z]\in A]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\sum_{u\in V(T_{0})}\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T}^{\prime},u,Z^{\prime}])]}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}_{0})]},

(11.2)

for any measurable set
A
.
Observe that
\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}_{0})]<\infty
because
\beta<1
, and the descendant trees of the children of
\mathbf{o}_{0}
(in
\mathbf{T}_{0}
) are subcritical Galton-Watson trees.

Recall that
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{*}
denotes the space of rooted marked Family Trees.
For any
[T,u,z]\in\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{*}
that has a unique bi-infinite
F
-path
\neswarrow(T)
, for any
w\in V(T)
, let

M(w):=\inf\{n\geq 0:F^{n}(w)\in\neswarrow(T)\},

with the convention that
F^{0}(w)=w
and let
w^{*}=F^{M(w)}(w)
be the smallest ancestor of
w
that lies on the unique bi-infinite
F
-path of
T
.
Let
[T,u,z]_{r}
denote the subtree induced by the vertices of
T
that are at a graph distance of at most
r
from
u
and with the mark function restricted to these vertices.

Proposition 82
.

The
F
-probability of the unimodularized
MEKT(\alpha,\beta)
is
MEKT(\alpha,\beta)
.

Proof.

Fix
r>0
.
Let
A
be a measurable subset of
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{*}
, and let
n>r
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]_{r}\in A]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]_{r}\in A,M(\mathbf{o})<n-r\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left[[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]_{r}\in A,M(\mathbf{o})\geq n-r\right].

The second sum is bounded by
\mathbb{P}[M(\mathbf{o})\geq n-r]
.
As
n\to\infty
,
\mathbb{P}[M(\mathbf{o})\geq n-r]\to 0
since
M(\mathbf{o})
is a.s. finite, which follows from the finiteness of the tree
\mathbf{T}_{0}
.
Therefore, the second sum tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
.

Consider the first sum.
We have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]_{r}\in A,

\displaystyle M(\mathbf{o})<n-r]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-r-1}\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]_{r}\in A,M(\mathbf{o})=k]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-r-1}\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T},F^{n-k}(\mathbf{o}^{*}),Z]_{r}\in A,M(\mathbf{o})=k].

The last equation follows by the definition of
\mathbf{o}^{*}:=F^{M(\mathbf{o})}(\mathbf{o})
.
By the definition of
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o},Z]
(see Eq. (
11.2
)), the last equation is equal to

\sum_{k=0}^{n-r-1}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in V(\mathbf{T}_{0})}\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T}^{\prime},F^{n-k}(u^{*}),Z^{\prime}]_{r})\mathbf{1}\{M(u)=k\}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}_{0})]}.

(11.3)

On the tree
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
, we have
F^{n-k}(u^{*})=\mathbf{o}_{n-k}
, for all
u\in V(\mathbf{T}_{0})
and for all
0\leq k\leq n-r-1
.
Since
([\mathbf{T}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i},Z_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an i.i.d. sequence, the distribution of
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}_{n-k},Z^{\prime}]_{r}
is independent of
[\mathbf{T}_{0},\mathbf{o}_{0},Z_{0}]
for all
0\leq k\leq n-r-1
.
By applying this fact to Eq. (
11.3
), it is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-r-1}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in V(\mathbf{T}_{0})}\mathbf{1}_{A}([\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}_{n-k},Z^{\prime}]_{r})\mathbf{1}\{M(u)=k\}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}_{0})]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-r-1}\frac{\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}_{n-k},Z^{\prime}]_{r}\in A]\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in V(\mathbf{T}_{0})}\mathbf{1}\{M(u)=k\}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[\#V(\mathbf{T}_{0})]}.

The distribution of
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}_{n-k},Z^{\prime}]_{r}
is identical to that of
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]_{r}
for all
n>2r+k
, by the construction of
MEKT(\alpha,\beta)
.
Using this and applying Eq. (
11.2
) to the last equation, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]_{r}\in A,M(\mathbf{o})<n-r\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]_{r}\in A]\sum_{k=0}^{n-r-1}\mathbb{P}[M(\mathbf{o})=k]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]_{r}\in A]\mathbb{P}[M(\mathbf{o})<n-r].

As
n\to\infty
,
\mathbb{P}[M(\mathbf{o})<n-r]\to 1
.
Therefore,

\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]_{r}\in A]\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathbb{P}[[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]_{r}\in A].

Since this is true for any
r\geq 1
, we have

[\mathbf{T},F^{n}(\mathbf{o}),Z]\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}].

∎

Remark 83
.

From the construction of
MEKT(\alpha,\beta)
, it follows that both
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},F(\mathbf{o}^{\prime}),Z^{\prime}]
and
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]
have the same distribution.
So, the marked EFT
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]
satisfies the Mecke’s invariant measure equation.
Therefore, by Lemma
66
,
\mathbf{o}^{\prime}
has a.s. infinitely many descendants, which can also be seen directly from the construction of
MEKT(\alpha,\beta)
.

Chapter 5
Record vertex-shift probability for the random walk case

In this chapter, we focus again on the record vertex-shift on the network
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
, where
X
is an i.i.d. sequence as in Def.
22
and satisfies
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
.
We show the existence of a unique
R
-probability (i.e., a vertex-shift probability under record vertex-shift) when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
.
Although one could show the same when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<0
, we did not consider it since the component of
0
can only give partial information of the trajectory of random walk associated to
X
.

We use the FB Lemma
61
to prove the existence and uniqueness of the
R
-probability.
We define two maps namely the backward map
\Phi_{R}
and the extended backward map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
that satisfy the hypotheses of the FB Lemma when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]>0
respectively.
This is done in several sections.
We also give constructions for the
R
-probability in these two cases and prove that the constructions indeed generate the
R
-probability.

(In French)
Dans ce chapitre, nous nous intéressons à nouveau au décalage de sommet des records sur le réseau
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
, où
X
est une suite i.i.d. comme dans la définition
22
et qui satisfait
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
.
Nous montrons l’existence d’une
R
-probabilité unique (c’est-à-dire une probabilité de décalage de sommet associée au décalage de sommet des records) lorsque
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
.
Bien que l’on puisse montrer la même chose lorsque
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<0
, nous ne l’avons pas considéré car la composante de
0
ne peut donner qu’une information partielle sur la trajectoire de la marche aléatoire associée à
X
.

Nous utilisons le lemme FB
61
pour prouver l’existence et l’unicité de la
R
-probabilité.
Nous définissons deux fonctions nommées la fonction réciproque
\Phi_{R}
et la fonction réciproque étendue
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
qui satisfont les hypothèses du lemme FB lorsque
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
et
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]>0
respectivement.
Ceci est fait dans plusieurs sections.
Nous donnons également des constructions pour la
R
-probabilité dans ces deux cas et prouvons que les constructions génèrent bien la
R
-probabilité.

12
The backward map of the record vertex-shift

In this section, we define a backward map
\Phi_{R}:\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}_{*}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}
is the subset of
\mathcal{T}_{*}
consisting of all ordered Family Trees that have at most one bi-infinite
F
-path.
Recall that the forward map
\Psi_{R}:\mathcal{G}_{*}\to\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}
maps a network of the form
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
to
[\mathbb{Z}^{R}(0),0]
, where
x
is a sample of the i.i.d. sequence
X
with
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
, and
\mathbb{Z}^{R}(0)
is the connected component of
0
of the record graph
\mathbb{Z}^{R}
of
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
.
For any ordered Family Tree that has at most one bi-infinite path, using the depth-first search order and the tree structure, we can define a sequence in such a way that the record graph associated to the sequence is the Family Tree.
This map essentially depends on a sequence of vertices of the Family Tree called succession line, which can be seen as a factor map from
\mathbb{Z}
to the vertices of a unimodular Family Tree (
[
11
]
).

This map extends the usual depth-first search encoding of finite ordered trees, defined in
[
14
, Chapter 5]
,
[
16
]
, to infinite ordered Family Trees that have at most one bi-infinite path.

In the next sections,we show that the map
\Phi_{R}
satisfies the three conditions of the FB Lemma
61
when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
However, this maps fails to be bijective when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]>0
.
So, in Section
14
, we extend this map to the space of marked Family Trees in order to obtain a bijective map.

In Subsection
5.2.2
of Chapter
3
, we show that the
R
-graph of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
is a unimodular ordered Eternal Galton-Watson Tree
EGWT(\pi)
with offspring distribution
\pi
, where
\pi
is the distribution of
X_{0}+1
.
The parent vertex-shift probability of
EGWT(\pi)
is shown to be the Eternal Kesten Tree
EKT(\pi)
(see Subsection
11.3
for the definition of
EKT(\pi)
).
In contrast to
EGWT(\pi)
, when
\pi(1)<1
, a realization of
EKT(\pi)
has a unique bi-infinite path.
So, the map
\Phi_{R}
should be defined such that it is continuous on the support of
EKT(\pi)
in order to satisfy the hypotheses of FB Lemma
61
.
Therefore, our first goal is to define
\Phi_{R}
on ordered Family Trees that have at most one bi-infinite path.
We achieve this goal using the properties of the RLS order.
Then, we show that the defined map
\Phi_{R}
is continuous
EKT(\pi)
-a.s..

Let
T
be an ordered Family Tree.
Consider the RLS order
\prec
on the vertices of
T
and recall that for any two vertices
u
and
v
, we say
u
succeeds
v
if
u\prec v
(equivalently
v
precedes
u
).

Definition 84
.

For any vertex
u\in T
, let
B(u):=\{v\in T:v\prec u\}
and define
b(u)=\max\{v\in B(u)\}
if it exists, where
\max
is taken with respect to
\prec
.
Similarly, let
A(u):=\{v\in T:v\succ u\}
and
a(u)=\min\{u\in A(u)\}
if it exists, where
\min
is taken with respect to
\prec
.

According to the RLS order,
b(u)
has the highest precedence over all those vertices that succeed
u
, and
a(u)
has the smallest precedence among those vertices that precede
u
.

Lemma 85
.

Let
T
be an ordered Family Tree and
u\in T
.
If
u
is not the smallest vertex, then
b(u)
exists.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
B(u)
is not empty.
So, either of the following two cases occur:

•

If
u
has children, then since it could only have finitely many children,
b(u)
is the largest(eldest) child of
u
.

•

If
u
does not have any child, let
w=F^{m}(u)
(for some
m\geq 1
) be the smallest ancestor of
u
that has a child smaller than
F^{m-1}(u)
, with the notation
F^{0}(u)=u
. Such an
m
exists because
B(u)
is non-empty.
Then,
b(u)
is the largest among those siblings of
F^{m-1}(u)
that are smaller than
F^{m-1}(u)
.

∎

Lemma 86
.

Let
T
be an ordered Family Tree,
u
be a non-maximal vertex of
T
.
If every sibling of
u
that precedes
u
has finitely many descendants, then
a(u)
exists.

Proof.

If
u
does not have a sibling that precedes
u
, then
a(u)=F(u)
(by hypothesis
F(u)\not=u
in this case).
Otherwise, let
w
be the smallest sibling of
u
larger than
u
.
It has finitely many descendants by hypothesis.
Then
a(u)
is the minimal vertex of
D(w)
(recall that
D(w)=\{v\in V(T)\backslash\{w\}:F^{m}(v)=w\text{ for some }m>0\}\cup\{w\}
).
∎

Definition 87
.

Consider an ordered rooted Eternal Family Tree
(T,o)
that has at most one bi-infinite
F
-path passing through
o
.
With the RLS order, we call
succession line
of
(T,o)
the sequence
U((T,o))=(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
iteratively given by
u_{0}=o
and,

\displaystyle u_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}b(u_{n+1})\text{ if it exists}\\
u_{n+1}\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}\text{ for $n<0$},

(12.1)

\displaystyle u_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a(u_{n-1})\text{ if it exists}\\
u_{n-1}\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}\text{ for $n>0$}.

Observe that this is the unique sequence that satisfies
u_{0}=o
, and
u_{n-1}
is the immediate successor of
u_{n}
, when they are distinct, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
With the above preparation, we are ready to define the backward map which plays a crucial role in the rest of the chapter.

Definition 88
.

Define the
backward map of the record vertex-shift

\Phi_{R}:\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}_{*}
as the function that maps
[T,o]\in\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}
to

\Phi_{R}([T,o])=[\mathbb{Z},0,x((T,o))],

(12.2)

where
x((T,o))=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is given by
x_{n}=d_{1}(u_{n+1})-1
and
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=U((T,o))
.

Note that
\Phi_{R}
is well-defined: if
(T,o)\sim(T^{\prime},o^{\prime})
be two isomorphic ordered Family Trees and
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
(u^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be their respective succession lines passing through
o,o^{\prime}
respectively, then
d_{1}(u_{n})-1=d_{1}(u_{n}^{\prime})-1
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, which implies that
x((T,o))=x((T^{\prime},o^{\prime}))
.
In general the succession line of a rooted Family Tree
[T,o]
passing through
o
may not contain all the vertices of
T
.
But, if
[T,o]
is a sample of component of
0
in the record graph of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
with
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
, then we show that the succession line of
[T,o]
contains every vertex of
T
(see Lemma
89
and Remark
90
).

Lemma 89
.

Let
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the i.i.d. increments of a skip-free to the left random walk with
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
and
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
be the network associated to
X
.
Let
[\mathbf{T},0]
be the component of
0
in the record graph of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
rooted at
0
.
Then, a.s., the succession line
U((\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}))
consists of distinct entries.

Proof.

Observe that the set of minimal vertices of
\mathbf{T}
is a covariant subset.
It can only be either empty or a singleton, because
\mathbf{T}
is totally ordered by the RLS order.
But
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
is unimodular (Lemma
16
), and
\#V(\mathbf{T})
is infinite since
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
, in fact
V(\mathbf{T})=\mathbb{Z}
since the record graph is connected when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
(see Proposition
40
and Proposition
41
).
Therefore, by No Infinite/Finite inclusion Lemma
12
, a.s., the minimal vertex does not exist.
Therefore, by Lemma
85
, almost surely, the successor of
u
,
b(u)
exists for all
u\in V(\mathbf{T})
.
Since
a(b(u))=u
for all
u\in V(\mathbf{T})
,
a(v)
exists for all
v
in the range of the map
b
.
But
b
is injective a.s. and hence surjective a.s. (by the unimodularity of
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
).
Therefore, the range of
b
is
V(\mathbf{T})
a.s. and hence, a.s.,
a(u)
exists for all
u\in V(\mathbf{T})
.
Thus, the map
n\mapsto u_{n}
from
\mathbb{Z}
to
V(\mathbf{T})
is injective, which implies that all the elements of the sequence
U((\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}))
are distinct.
∎

Remark 90
.

Observe that
U(\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,X]))=(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=(n)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, when the i.i.d. sequence
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
.
This follows by combining Lemma
33
with the facts that the
R
-graph
\mathbb{Z}^{R}
of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
is a.s. connected, and the map
n\mapsto u_{n}
from
\mathbb{Z}
to
V(\mathbb{Z}^{R})=\mathbb{Z}
is injective.
Indeed, by definition,
B(0)=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:i\prec 0\}
.
But, by Lemma
33
,
i\in B(0)\iff i<0
.
Therefore,
B(0)=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:i<0\}
.
Hence,
b(0)=-1
and
u_{-1}=-1
.
By induction, assume
u_{-n}=-n
.
Consider
B(u_{-n})=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:i\prec-n\}=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:i<-n\}
.
Therefore,
u_{-(n+1)}=-(n+1)
.
Similarly, we could show that
a(u_{n})=n,\forall n\geq 0
.

Lemma 91
.

Let
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}]
be a random ordered Family Tree whose distribution is
EKT(\pi)
, where
\pi
is a probability distribution on
\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
with mean
m(\pi)=1
.
Then, the succession line
U((\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}))
consists of distinct entries.

Proof.

Let
[T^{\prime},o^{\prime}]
be a realization of
EKT(\pi)
.
Since
T^{\prime}
has a unique bi-infinite
F
-path passing through the root
o
, by Lemma
85
,
b(u)
exists for all
u\in V(T^{\prime})
.
If
u
satisfies the property that every sibling of
u
that precedes
u
has finitely many descendants, then, by Lemma
86
,
a(u)
exists.
In this case,
a(u)
also satisfies the same property.
Since the root
o
satisfies this property, the map
n\mapsto u_{n}
from
\mathbb{Z}
to
V(T^{\prime})
is injective.
Thus, all the elements of
U(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})
are distinct.
∎

The following remark is used in the later sections to describe the
R
-probability from
EKT
.

Remark 92
.

Let
[T^{\prime},o^{\prime}]
be a realization of
EKT(\pi)
and
\neswarrow([T^{\prime},o^{\prime}])
denote its bi-infinite
F
-path.
Since
o^{\prime}\in\neswarrow([T^{\prime},o^{\prime}])
, we could define the following.
The
positive part
of
[T^{\prime},o^{\prime}]
is the rooted Family Tree
[T^{\prime+},o^{\prime}]
, where
T^{\prime+}
is the subtree of
T^{\prime}
induced by the vertices
\{u\in V(T^{\prime}):u\succ v\text{ for some }v\in\neswarrow([T^{\prime},o^{\prime}])\}
.
Observe that the set of elements of
U((T^{\prime},o^{\prime}))
is equal to
V(T^{\prime})
.

13
Bijectivity and continuity of the backward map

We first show that
\Psi_{R}\circ\Phi_{R}([T,o])=[T,o]
a.s. for every realization
[T,o]
of the ordered
EGWT(\pi)
, where the mean
m(\pi)=1
, using the following lemma.

Lemma 93
.

Let
T
be a finite ordered tree and
v_{1}\prec v_{2}\prec\cdots\prec v_{n}
be the vertices of
T
ordered according to the RLS order. Then,

1.

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)=-1
.

2.

\sum_{i=1}^{k}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)<0
, for all
1<k\leq n
.

Proof.

The first equation follows from the fact that for a finite tree
T
,

\sum_{u\in V(T)}d_{1}(u)=\#V(T)-1.

We prove the second equation by induction on
k
.
If
k=1
then
v_{1}
is a leaf and hence
d_{1}(v_{1})-1=-1
.
Now, assume that the statement is true for
1\leq m\leq k
, where
k>1
.
Observe that by the RLS order, either
v_{k+1}
is a leaf or
v_{k+1}
is the parent of
v_{k}
.
In the latter case, there exists
1\leq j\leq k
such that
v_{j}<v_{j+1}<\cdots<v_{k}
(written according to the RLS order) are all the descendants of
v_{k+1}
.
If
v_{k+1}
is a leaf then, by induction,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)+d_{1}(v_{k+1})-1<-1.

The only other possibility is that
v_{k+1}
is the parent of
v_{k}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)+\sum_{i=j}^{k+1}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)\leq 0+(-1).

The last step follows from the fact that the first sum on the right-hand side is at most
0
(by induction), while the second sum is on the descendant tree of
v_{k+1}
which is equal to
-1
by the first result of the lemma.
∎

Remark 94
.

Let
v_{1}\prec v_{2}\prec\cdots\prec v_{n}
be the vertices of a finite ordered tree, ordered according to RLS order.
Then using Lemma
93
we obtain, for any
1<k<n
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)-\sum_{i=1}^{k}(d_{1}(v_{i})-1)\geq-1-(-1)\geq 0.

Proposition 95
.

Almost surely, every realisation
[T,o]
of the ordered

EGWT(\pi)
, where the mean
m(\pi)=1
, satisfies
\Psi_{R}\circ\Phi_{R}([T,o])=[T,o]
.

Proof.

Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=U((T,o))
be the succession line of
(T,o)
and
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]=\Phi_{R}([T,o])
, where
x=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is such that
x_{n}=d_{1}(u_{n})-1
is the mark of the edge
(n,n+1)
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

First, observe that the map
\alpha:\mathbb{Z}\to V(T)
defined by
\alpha(n)=u_{n}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is bijective.
This follows because, every vertex
v
of
T
has finitely many descendants.
So, there are only finitely many vertices between
o
and
v
according to the RLS order, and hence
u_{n}=v
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore,
\alpha
is surjective.
The injectivity of the map
\alpha
follows from Lemma
89
because
[T,o]
is a realisation of the component of
0
in the record graph of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
We now prove that the bijective map
\alpha:\mathbb{Z}\to V(T)
defined by
\alpha(n)=u_{n}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, induces a rooted network isomorphism
\alpha
from
\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])
to
(T,o)
.
Note that, if
u_{j}
is a parent of
u_{i}
in
T
for some integers
i
and
j
then
j>i
, which follows because
(u_{n})_{\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the succession line of
(T,o)
.
In view of this, to prove that
\alpha
induces a rooted network isomorphism, it is enough to show that for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
u_{j}
is the parent of
u_{i}
in
T
for some
j>i
, then
j=R_{x}(i)
(since every vertex has at most one parent).

Let
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
u_{j}
be the parent of
u_{i}
in
T
for some integer
j>i
.
If
j=i+1
, then
x_{i}=d_{1}(u_{i+1})-1\geq 0
, because
u_{i}
is a child of
u_{i+1}
.
Hence,
R_{x}(i)=i+1
.

So, let us assume that
j>i+1
.
This implies that either
u_{i+1}
is a leaf and a descendant of a sibling of
u_{i}
, or
u_{i+1}
is a leaf and a sibling of
u_{i}
.
The descendant tree of
u_{j}
is a finite ordered tree, as
[T,o]
is a realization of
EGWT(\pi)
.
Let
u_{i_{1}}\prec u_{i_{2}}\prec\cdots\prec u_{i_{n}}
be all the elder siblings of
u_{i}
, and
T_{1},T_{2},\cdots,T_{n}
be their descendant trees respectively.
For any
i<k<j
, let
l(k)
be the smallest element of the set
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
such that
\{u_{i+1},u_{i+2},\cdots,u_{k}\}\subset V(T_{1})\cup V(T_{2})\cup\cdots V(T_{l(k)})
, and
u_{k_{1}}
be the smallest vertex of
T_{l(k)}
.

Then, the sum
y(i,k)=\sum_{m=i}^{k-1}x_{m}=\sum_{m=i+1}^{k}(d_{1}(u_{m})-1)
can be written as

\displaystyle y(i,k)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{u\in V(T_{1})}d_{1}(u)-1\right)+\cdots+\left(\sum_{u\in V(T_{l(k)-1})}d_{1}(u)-1\right)+\sum_{m=k_{1}}^{k}(d_{1}(u_{m})-1)

\displaystyle<(-1)+\cdots+(-1)+\sum_{m=k_{1}}^{k}(d_{1}(u_{m})-1)<0,

with the notation that
V(T_{0})
is just an empty set.
The last steps follow from the first and the second statements of Lemma
93
.

On the other hand, the sum
y(i,j)=\sum_{m=i+1}^{j}(d_{1}(u_{m})-1)
can be written as

\displaystyle y(i,j)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{u\in V(T_{1})}d_{1}(u)-1\right)+\cdots+\left(\sum_{u\in V(T_{n})}d_{1}(u)-1\right)+d_{1}(u_{j})-1

\displaystyle=-n+d_{1}(u_{j})-1\geq 0.

The above steps follow from the first part of Lemma
93
, and by the assumption that
u_{j}
has at least
n+1
children, namely
u_{i},u_{i_{1}},\cdots,u_{i_{n}}
.
Thus,
R_{x}(i)=j
, completing the proof.
∎

The following proposition shows that the backward map
\Phi_{R}
satisfies the first condition of the FB lemma (Lemma
61
).

Proposition 96
.

Let
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be as in Def.
22
and satisfies
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
Let
x=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a realization of
X
, and
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
be its associated network.
Then,
\Phi_{R}\circ\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])=[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
.

Proof.

Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=U(\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x]))
be the succession line of
\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])
.
By Remark
90
,
u_{n}=n
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
[\mathbb{Z},0,y]:=\Phi_{R}\circ\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])
, where
y=(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
y_{n}:=d_{1}(u_{n+1})-1=d_{1}(n+1)-1
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
From the first statement of Lemma
34
,
x_{n}=d_{1}(n+1)-1
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore,
x_{n}=y_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

We now show that the map
\Phi_{R}
is continuous on the set of ordered family trees that have at most one bi-infinite
F
-path.

Lemma 97
.

Let
[T,o]
be an ordered EFT that has at most one bi-infinite
F
-path, and such that the bi-infinite path passes through
o
.
If
([T_{n},o_{n}])_{n\geq 1}
is any sequence of rooted Family Trees such that
[T_{n},o_{n}]\rightarrow[T,o]
as
n\to\infty
, then
\Phi_{R}([T_{n},o_{n}])\rightarrow\Phi_{R}([T,o])
as
n\to\infty
(both convergences are in local sense).

Proof.

For
r>0
, denote
x_{r}((T,o))=(x_{-r},x_{-r+1},\cdots,x_{0},\cdots,x_{r-1},x_{r})
.
We need to show that given
r>0
, there exists
N_{0}
such that for all
n>N_{0}
,
x_{r}((T_{n},o_{n}))=x_{r}((T,o))
.
Since
(T_{n},o_{n})\rightarrow(T,o)
, it is enough to show that, given
r>0
, there exists
R>0
such that
x_{r}((T,o)_{R})=x_{r}(T,o)
, where
(T,o)_{R}
is the restriction of
T
to the graph ball of radius
R
centered at
o
.

Let
(o_{-r-1},o_{-r},\cdots,o,o_{1},\cdots,o_{r+1})
be the
r+1
restriction of the
F
-bi-infinite path of
(T,o)
passing through
o
.
If such a path does not exist (take for instance, a sample of the non-trivial unimodular EGWT), then we can find the largest
k<0
such that
o_{l}=o_{k}
for all
-r-1\leq l\leq k
.

Let
h_{i}
be the largest graph distance from
o_{i}
to the descendants of
o_{i}
which are not the descendants of
o_{i-1}
for
-r-1\leq i\leq r+1
.
Then,
h_{i}
is finite for all
i
since the descendants of
o_{i}
that are not the descendants of
o_{i-1}
are finite for all
i
(otherwise this contradicts the fact that
T
has at most one bi-infinite
F
-path).
Take
R=\max\{h_{i}:-r-1\leq i\leq r+1\}
.
Then, we have
U((T,o)_{R})=U((T,o))
.
Thus,
x_{r}((T,o)_{R})=x_{r}((T,o))
.
∎

14
The extended backward map of record vertex-shift

In this section, we define a map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
on the space of marked Family Trees and show that it satisfies all the conditions of FB Lemma
61
.
The map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
will be used later to show that the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
exists.
We call the map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}

the extended backward map
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}
(resp.
\hat{\mathcal{T}_{*}}^{\prime}
) be the space of rooted ordered Family Trees (resp. space of rooted marked ordered Family Trees whose marks take values in
\mathbb{Z}
) that have at most one bi-infinite
F
-path.

We have defined earlier in Eq. (
12.1
) the succession line of a rooted ordered Family Tree
[T,o]
that has at most one bi-infinite
F
-path, and using this we defined in Eq. (
12.2
) the backward map
\Phi_{R}:\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{*}
.

In order to use the FB lemma (Lemma
61
), we need this backward map to satisfy

\Phi_{R}\circ\Psi_{R}=I\ \mathcal{P}\text{-a.s.},

(14.1)

where
\mathcal{P}
is the distribution of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
, where
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]\geq 0
.

When
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]>0
, the random walk
(S_{-n})_{n\geq 0}
starting at
0
drifts to
-\infty
(where
S_{-n}=\sum_{k=-n}^{-1}-X_{k}
for all
n>0
and
S_{0}=0
).
In this case,
\Phi_{R}
does not satisfy Eq. (
14.1
) since
\Psi_{R}
fails to be injective.
For instance, consider the set
A=\{[\mathbb{Z},0,x=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}]:x_{-1}=3\text{ and }s_{-n}\to-\infty\text{ as }n\to\infty\}
.
The set
A
has positive measure since we assumed that
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]>0
.
For each
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]\in A
, define a new element
[\mathbb{Z},0,\tilde{x}]
, where the sequence
\tilde{x}=(\tilde{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is given by the following:
\tilde{x}_{n}=x_{n}
for all
n\not=-1
and
\tilde{x}_{-1}=4
.
In comparison to the sums
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
associated to
x
, the sums
(\tilde{s}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
associated to the sequence
\tilde{x}
are shifted by
-1
for all
n\leq-1
.
Both the networks
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
and
[\mathbb{Z},0,\tilde{x}]
have the same record graphs.
So,
\Psi_{R}
is not injective with positive probability, see Figure
10
.

Figure 10:
Example showing that when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]>0
, two samples of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
can have the same record graph.

To obtain the injectivity, we enrich the record graph
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
with the type function of
X
as a mark function on the vertices of the record graph.
This gives the forward map
\hat{\Psi}_{R}:\mathcal{G}_{*}\to\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{*}^{\prime}
given by
\hat{\Psi}_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])=[T,0,t]
, where
[T,0]=\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])
is the component of
0
in the record graph of
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
,
t
is the type function associated to
x
, and
x=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sample of the i.i.d. sequence
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{{Z}}}
when
0<\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<\infty
.
We define a backward map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
(associated to
X
) on
\hat{\mathcal{T}_{*}}^{\prime}
such that it satisfies Eq. (
14.1
) with
\hat{\Psi}_{R}
as the forward map.

Definition 98
.

Let
[T,o,M]
be a rooted marked Family Tree that has at most one bi-infinite
F
-path and
M:V(T)\to\mathbb{Z}
be a marked function such that
M(u)\geq-1
for all
u\in V(T)
.
Let
u((T,o,M))=(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be its succession line with
u_{0}=o
.
Define

\hat{\Phi}_{R}([T,o,M])=[\mathbb{Z},0,x],

(14.2)

where
x=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is given by
x_{n}=(d_{1}(u_{n+1})-1)\mathbf{1}\{u_{n+1}\not\in\neswarrow\}+(d_{1}(u_{n+1})+M(u_{n+1})-1)\mathbf{1}\{u_{n+1}\in\neswarrow\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
is defined keeping in mind the relation between increment, offspring count and type as given in Lemma
38
.

15
Bijectivity and continuity of the extended backward map

Let
\hat{\Psi}_{R}:\mathcal{G}_{*}\to\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{*}^{\prime}
be the forward map given by
\hat{\Psi}_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])=[T,0,t]
, where
[T,0]=\Psi_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])
is the component of
0
in the record graph of
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
,
t
is the type function associated to
x
(see subsection
4.5
for the definition of the type function), and
x=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sample of the i.i.d. sequence
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{{Z}}}
when
0<\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<\infty
.

Proposition 99
.

Let
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be an i.i.d. sequence as in Def.
22
with
0<\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<\infty
, and
\mathcal{P}
be the distribution of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
on
\mathcal{G}_{*}
.
Then,
\hat{\Phi}_{R}\circ\hat{\Psi}_{R}=I\ \mathcal{P}-a.s.

Proof.

Let
x=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sample of
X
,
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]
be its network, and let
[T,0,t]=\hat{\Psi}_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,x])
be the marked random rooted tree, where the mark function is give by the type function
t
associated to
x
.
We know that
[T,0,t]
has at most one bi-infinite path and
T
is eternal.
So, we obtain its succession
u([T,0,t])=(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
Note that
u_{n}=n
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by Remark
90
.
Let
[\mathbb{Z},0,y]=\hat{\Phi}_{R}([T,0,t])
, where
y=(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the sequence given by
y_{n}=(d_{1}(u_{n+1})-1)\mathbf{1}\{u_{n+1}\not\in\neswarrow\}+(d_{1}(u_{n+1})+t_{x}(u_{n+1})-1)\mathbf{1}\{u_{n+1}\in\neswarrow\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By Lemma
38
and the fact that for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
u_{n}\in\neswarrow
if and only if
t(u_{n})>-1
, it follows that
x_{n}=(d_{1}(n+1)-1)\mathbf{1}\{t_{x}(n+1)=-1\}+(d_{1}(n+1)+t(n+1)-1)\mathbf{1}\{t(n+1)\geq 0\}=y_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus,
[\mathbb{Z},0,x]=[\mathbb{Z},0,y]
.
∎

Lemma 100
.

Let
[T,o,m]\in\hat{\mathcal{T}_{*}}^{\prime}
.
If a sequence
([T_{n},o_{n},m_{n}])_{n\geq 1}
in
\hat{\mathcal{T}_{*}}^{\prime}
converges to
[T,o,m]
as
n\to\infty
, then
\hat{\Phi}_{R}([T_{n},o_{n},m_{n}])
converges to
\hat{\Phi}_{R}([T,o,t])
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Since the marks take values in
\mathbb{Z}
, which is a discrete set, the proof of the statement is the same as in Lemma
97
, but the underlined space is
\hat{\mathcal{T}_{*}}^{\prime}
instead of
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\prime}
.
∎

16
Existence of R-probability for non-negative mean

Let
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the i.i.d. sequence of increments of a skip-free to the left random walk and let
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
be its associated network.
Let
\pi
be the distribution of
X_{0}+1
.

Theorem 101
.

If
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
, then the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
exists.
Its distribution is equal to the push-forward of
EKT(\pi)
under the map
\Phi_{R}
, where
\pi
is the probability distribution of
X_{0}+1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the distribution of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
, and
\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{P}\circ\Psi_{R}^{-1}
.
We show that
\mathcal{P}
and
\Phi_{R}
satisfy all the hypotheses of FB Lemma
61
.
Theorem
53
shows that
\mathcal{Q}=EGWT(\pi)
, where the offspring distribution
\pi\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}X_{0}+1
.
Proposition
96
implies the first condition of Lemma
61
.
In Lemma
80
, we showed that the
F
-probability
\mathcal{Q}_{F}
of
EGWT(\pi)
exists and it is equal to
EKT(\pi)
, satisfying the second condition of Lemma
61
.
The third condition of Lemma
61
is shown to be satisfied by
\Phi_{R}
in Lemma
97
.
Thus, the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
exists, and its distribution is
\mathcal{Q}_{F}\circ\Phi_{R}^{-1}
.
∎

Assume
0<\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<\infty
.
Let
\bar{\pi}
and
\tilde{\pi}
be the probability distributions defined in Eq. (
5.8
).
Consider the bi-variate ECS ordered marked Eternal Kesten Tree
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})
whose marked bush has the distribution given in Theorem
55
.

Theorem 102
.

If
0<\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<\infty
, then the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
exists, and its distribution is
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})\circ\hat{\Phi}_{R}^{-1}
, the push-forward measure of
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})
under the extended backward map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
.

Proof.

Let
[\mathbf{T},0,t]=\hat{\Psi}_{R}([\mathbb{Z},0,X])
, where
t
is the type function associated to
X
, and
\mathcal{P}
be the distribution of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
.
We apply FB Lemma
61
.
By Proposition
99
, the extended map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}\circ\hat{\Psi}_{R}=Id\ \mathcal{P}-a.s.
.
By Theorem
55
,
[\mathbf{T},0,t]
is the unimodularised
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})
(in the sense of Eq. (
3.3
)).
By Proposition
82
,
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})
is the
F
-probability of the unimodularised
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})
.
By Lemma
100
, the extended backward map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
is continuous.

So, all the three conditions of the FB lemma (Lemma
61
) are satisfied by the extended backward map
\hat{\Phi}_{R}
and the probability measure
\mathcal{P}
.
Thus,
R
-probability
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
exists, and its distribution is equal to
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})\circ\hat{\Phi}_{R}^{-1}
.
∎

Remark 103
.

When
0<\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]<\infty
,
MEKT(\bar{\pi},\tilde{\pi})
is the distribution of the record graph of the conditioned network
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
conditioned on
\{S_{n}\leq 0,\forall n\leq 0\}
which is equivalent to
\{0\in\neswarrow\}
(i.e.,
0
belongs to the bi-infinite path of the record graph) by Theorem
55
.
This implies that the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
is the conditioned network
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
conditioned on
\{S_{n}\leq 0,\forall n\leq 0\}
.

17
Construction of the R-probability for zero mean

We have seen in the previous sections that the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
exists when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
We now provide two constructions for the
R
-probability and prove in Proposition
104
that the constructions indeed generate the
R
-probability when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
The second construction is useful to prove several properties of the
R
-probability, see Section
18
.

Construction 1:
We now construct a sequence of random variables
Y=(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and show, in Proposition
104
, that its associated network
[\mathbb{Z},0,Y]
is the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
In particular, we show that
[\mathbb{Z},0,Y]=\Phi_{R}([\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}])
, where
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
is distributed as
EKT(\pi)
with
\pi
being the distribution of
X_{0}+1
.

Define the non-negatively indexed subsequence
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 0}
to be i.i.d. random variables with
Y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}X_{n},n\geq 0
.
The construction for the negatively indexed subsequence
(Y_{n})_{n<0}
is given in the following steps.
Recall that the size-biased distribution
\hat{\pi}
of
\pi
is given by
\hat{\pi}(k)=k\pi(k),k\in\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
.
In particular,
\hat{\pi}(\{1,2,3,\cdots\})=1
.

Let
\lambda
be the distribution of
X_{0}
.

1.

Let
l=-1
.

2.

Generate a random variable
Z
with distribution
\hat{\pi}
and assign
Y_{l}:=(Z-1)
.

3.

Let
K
be an integer chosen uniformly among
\{0,1,\cdots,Y_{l}\}
.

4.

Generate an i.i.d. sequence of random variables
(Z_{n})_{n<0}
with distribution
\lambda
.

5.

Let
\tau=\sup\{n<0:-Y_{l}-\sum_{m=n}^{-1}Z_{m}=-K\}
. Assign
Y_{n+l}:=Z_{n}
for
\tau\leq n<0
.

6.

Assign
l:=l+\tau-1
.

7.

Repeat the procedure from step 2.

Let
\tau_{n},l_{n}
denote
\tau
and
l
of the
n
-th (
n\geq 1
) iteration in the above construction, with the notation that
\tau_{0}=0,l_{0}=0
.
Then, for all
n\geq 2
, the following equality of joint distribution holds because of the iterative nature of the construction:

(Y_{\tau_{n}+l_{n}},Y_{\tau_{n}+l_{n}+1},\cdots,Y_{\tau_{n-1}+l_{n-1}-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}(Y_{\tau_{n-1}+l_{n-1}},Y_{\tau_{n-1}+l_{n-1}+1},\cdots,Y_{\tau_{n-2}+l_{n-2}-1}).

(17.1)

Figure 11:
Distribution of
EKT(\pi)
and its succession line starting from the root
\mathbf{o}
(drawn in blue).

Figure 12:
Distribution of
EGWT(\pi)
and its succession line starting from the root
\mathbf{o}
(drawn in blue).

Figure 13:
Sample trajectory of the sums of the
R
-probability sequence when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
(the
R
-probability sequence
Y=(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the sequence of increments of this trajectory).

Proposition 104
.

Let
[\mathbb{Z},0,Y]
be the network associated to the sequence of random variables
Y=(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
obtained from Construction 1.
Then
[\mathbb{Z},0,Y]
is the
R
-probability of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
, where
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is as in Def.
22
with
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be the distribution of
X_{0}+1
,
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
and
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}]
be random rooted trees with distributions
EKT(\pi)
(see Fig.
11
) and
EGWT(\pi)
(see Fig.
12
) respectively.
We show that
[\mathbb{Z},0,Y]=\Phi_{R}([\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}])
.
Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
(w_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the succession lines of
[\mathbf{T},\mathbf{o}]
and
[\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}]
respectively, with
u_{0}=\mathbf{o}
and
w_{0}=\mathbf{o}^{\prime}
.
It follows from the constructions of
EGWT(\pi)
and
EKT(\pi)
that the distribution of the sequence of offspring counts of
(u_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is the same as that of
(w_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.
Since the map
\Phi_{R}
is bijective
EGWT(\pi)
-a.s., by Proposition
96
we have
(d_{1}(w_{n})-1)_{n\geq 1}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(X_{n-1})_{n\geq 1}
.
Therefore,
(d_{1}(u_{n})-1)_{n\geq 1}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(d_{1}(w_{n})-1)_{n\geq 1}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(X_{n-1})_{n\geq 1}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(Y_{n-1})_{n\geq 1}
.

We now show that
(Y_{-n})_{n\geq 1}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(d_{1}(u_{-n+1})-1)_{n\geq 1}
.
Consider the random variable
\tau
defined in the first iteration of Construction 1.
Let
N
be a positive random variable such that
u_{-N}
is the unique child of
\mathbf{o}
that belongs to the bi-infinite path of
\mathbf{T}
, i.e.,
u_{-N}=\mathbf{o}_{-1}
.
By the construction of
EKT(\pi)
,
(d_{1}(u_{-N-n}))_{n\geq 0}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(d_{1}(u_{-n}))_{n\geq 0}
.
Therefore, by Eq. (
17.1
), it is enough to show that

\tau\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}-N+1

(17.2)

and the equality of the following conditioned distributions

\left(Y_{-n},Y_{-n+1},\cdots,Y_{-1}\right)|_{\tau=-n+1}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}\left(d_{1}(u_{-n+1})-1,d_{1}(u_{-n+2})-1,\cdots,d_{1}(u_{0})-1\right)|_{N=n}.

(17.3)

By the construction of
EKT(\pi)
and Construction 1, Eq. (
17.3
) follows.
It remains to show Eq. (
17.2
).
We show it using the following inequality.
For a realization
[T,o]
of
EKT(\pi)
, and any
M
such that
0<M\leq N-1
(
N
as above), let
u^{\prime}:=u^{\prime}(M)
denote the youngest child of
u_{0}
such that
u\succ u^{\prime}
implies that
u\in\{u_{0},u_{-1},\cdots,u_{-M}\}
.
Then, we have

\sum_{i=0}^{M}(d_{1}(u_{-i})-1)\geq\#\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\prec u_{-N}\},

(17.4)

with equality if and only if
M=N-1
.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{M}(d_{1}(u_{-i})-1)=

\displaystyle d_{1}(u_{0})-1

\displaystyle+\sum_{\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\succ u^{\prime}\}}\sum_{v\in T_{u}}(d_{1}(v)-1)+\sum_{\{u\in T_{u^{\prime}}:u\succeq u_{-M}\}}(d_{1}(u)-1),

where
T_{u}
in the above equation denotes the descendant tree of
u
(with root as
u
).
The above equation is obtained by rearranging the sum on the left-hand side into sums on the descendant trees of the children of
u_{0}
whose descendants are contained in
\{u_{0},u_{-1},\cdots,u_{-M}\}
and the remaining part.
By applying part 1 of Lemma
93
to the second sum and Remark
94
to the third sum of the above equation respectively, the right-hand side of the above equation is

\displaystyle\geq d_{1}(u_{0})-1+\sum_{\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\succ u^{\prime}\}}(-1)+0.

Since
u^{\prime}\succeq u_{-N}
, the sum in the above equation is lower bounded by
\#\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\succ u_{-N}\}
, so we obtain,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{M}(d_{1}(u_{-i})-1)

\displaystyle\geq d_{1}(u_{0})-1-\#\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\succ u_{-N}\}

\displaystyle=\#\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\prec u_{-N}\}.

Equality in the above occurs if and only if
u^{\prime}=u_{-N}
, i.e.,
M=N-1
.

Since the order of
\mathbf{o}_{-1}
(
=u_{-N}
) is uniform among the children of
\mathbf{o}
in
EKT(\pi)
, and
d_{1}(u_{0})>0
(because
d_{1}(u_{0})\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}\hat{\pi}
and
\hat{\pi}(\mathbb{N})=1
), it follows that the distribution of
\#\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\prec u_{-N}\}
conditioned on
\{d_{1}(u_{0})=\ell\}
is the uniform distribution on
\{0,1,2,\cdots,\ell-1\}
.
Therefore,
\#\{u\in D_{1}(u_{0}):u\prec u_{-N}\}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}K
, where
K
is the random variable in the first iteration of Construction 1.

By the argument following Eq. (
17.4
), it follows that
M=N-1
is the smallest non-negative integer for which equality is attained in Eq. (
17.4
).
Therefore,
\tau\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}-(N-1)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\left(Y_{-N},Y_{-N+1},\cdots,Y_{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(Z_{\tau},Z_{\tau+1},\cdots,Z_{-1},Y_{-1})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(d_{1}(u_{-N+1})-1,d_{1}(u_{-N+2})-1,\cdots,d_{1}(u_{0})-1).

∎

Construction 2:
In the following steps, we provide an alternative construction of the sequence
Y=(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
(also illustrated in Figure
13
) from the sequence
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where
X
is as in Def.
22
with
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
This construction is used in Section
20
to compare the foil structures of
[\mathbb{Z},0,X]
and
[\mathbb{Z},0,Y]
under the record vertex-shift.
Let
\pi
be the distribution of
X_{0}+1
, and
\hat{\pi}
be the size-biased distribution of
\pi
.

1.

Take
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.

2.

Let
(Z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an i.i.d. sequence of random variables, independent of
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, that take values in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
and whose common distribution is given by
\mathbb{P}[Z_{1}=b]=\hat{\pi}(b+1)
for all
b\geq-1
. Note that
\mathbb{P}[Z_{1}=-1]=0
.

3.

Let
(K_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an i.i.d. sequence of random variables such that
K_{n}
is independent of
Z_{m}
whenever
m\not=n
, and
\mathbb{P}[K_{n}=a|Z_{n}=b]=\frac{1}{b+1}\mathbf{1}_{0\leq a\leq b}
, for all
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and for all
n\geq 1
.

4.

Consider an array
(X_{m}^{(n)})_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
of i.i.d. random variables, independent of all the above random variables, such that
X_{1}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}X_{1}
.

5.

Define a sequence of random times
(\tau_{n})_{n\geq 1}
by
\tau_{n}:=\inf\{m\geq 1:-Z_{n}-\sum_{l=1}^{m}X_{m}^{(n)}\geq-K_{n}\}
for all
n\geq 1
. Note that
\tau_{n}<\infty
almost surely for all
n\geq 1
by Chung-Fuchs theorem. Since
X_{1}^{(1)}
cannot be smaller than
-1
, we have
-Z_{n}-\sum_{l=1}^{\tau_{n}}X_{m}^{(n)}=-K_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
.

Finally, consider the following independent sequence of random-sized tuples

\displaystyle(Y_{-1},Y_{-2},\cdots,Y_{-(\tau_{1}+1)})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}(Z_{1},X_{1}^{(1)},X_{2}^{(1)},\cdots,X_{\tau_{1}}^{(1)}),

\displaystyle(Y_{-(\tau_{1}+2)},\cdots,Y_{-(\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+2)})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}(Z_{2},X_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,X_{\tau_{2}}^{(2)}),\cdots,

\displaystyle(Y_{-(\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{n-1}+n)}+\cdots+Y_{-(\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{n-1}+\tau_{n}+n)})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}(Z_{n},X_{1}^{n},\cdots,X_{\tau_{n}}^{(n)}),\cdots.

We call the sequence
Y=(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
the
R
-probability sequence
associated to
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Remark 105
.

Note that
K_{1}
is a proper random variable since
\mathbb{P}[K_{1}=a]=\sum_{l=a+1}^{\infty}\pi(l)
for all non-negative integers
a\geq 0
and
\mathbb{P}[K_{n}<\infty]=\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\pi((a,\infty))=m(\pi)=1
.
Observe that
\mathbb{P}[K_{1}>0]>0
since
\pi(0)<1
.

From the construction, it is clear that
(K_{n},\tau_{n})\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(K,\tau)
, where
K,\tau
are the random variables defined in Construction 1.

18
Properties of the R-probability sequence for zero mean

In this section, we discuss the properties of the R-probability sequence
Y=(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
X=(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, defined in the last section, when
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=0
.
Recall that
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is an i.i.d. sequence whose common distribution is given by
Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}X_{0}
.
So, we focus on the properties of
(Y_{n})_{n<0}
.

For two integers
m<n
, let
w(m,n):=\sum_{k=m}^{n-1}Y_{k}
and
W_{n}=-w(-n,0)=-\sum_{k=-n}^{-1}Y_{k}
, for
n\geq 1
.
We continue to use the notations of the last section in the following lemma and proposition.
From the definitions of
\tau_{n}
and
K_{n}
, it follows that
W_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\cdots+\tau_{n}+n}=-(K_{1}+K_{2}+\cdots+K_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
.

Lemma 106
.

Almost surely
W_{m}<-(K_{1}+\cdots+K_{n}+K_{n+1})
for all positive integers
m,n
such that
n\geq 1
and
